Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.

It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were convinced that the spot was completely even and normal-looking?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I actually believed it was completely normal... I guess I wouldn't need to look at it. But it's kind of hard to even imagine feeling that way right now. So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were convinced that the spot was completely even and normal-looking?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I actually believed it was completely normal... I guess I wouldn't need to look at it. But it's kind of hard to even imagine feeling that way right now. So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were convinced that the spot was completely even and normal-looking?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I actually believed it was completely normal... I guess I wouldn't need to look at it. But it's kind of hard to even imagine feeling that way right now. So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your need to check your face in the mirror is more about making sure you look okay, or is it something you feel compelled to do regardless of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. I'll spend time trying to fix my hair around it to hide the spot, or I'll just sit there comparing myself to the girls who come through my line at the register. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your need to check your face in the mirror is more about making sure you look okay, or is it something you feel compelled to do regardless of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. I'll spend time trying to fix my hair around it to hide the spot, or I'll just sit there comparing myself to the girls who come through my line at the register. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were convinced that the spot was completely even and normal-looking?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I actually believed it was completely normal... I guess I wouldn't need to look at it. But it's kind of hard to even imagine feeling that way right now. So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your need to check your face in the mirror is more about making sure you look okay, or is it something you feel compelled to do regardless of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. I'll spend time trying to fix my hair around it to hide the spot, or I'll just sit there comparing myself to the girls who come through my line at the register. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were under the impression that the spot had been completely removed?\n\nPatient: I think... if I really believed it was totally gone, I wouldn't need to check. But it's hard to say for sure, because I'm just so used to doing it at this point.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, I came in because I just keep getting stuck on this... this tiny uneven spot right around my nose and cheek. I notice it a lot in photos, and especially under the bright fluorescent lights at the store where I work. Even when other people tell me they don't see it, it just feels like it's the very first thing the customers and my managers notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about the spot on your face affecting your daily activities or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. Um, my manager has actually noticed that I'm coming back from my breaks late lately. And I guess I'm moving slower at the register, too. I'm just really worried that it's making me look unreliable, especially right now when I'm trying to get promoted.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one spot on your face that you're concerned about, or are there other areas of your appearance that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. It's honestly just this one spot. The rest of my face and everything else is... fine, I guess. I don't really worry about any other parts of how I look. It's just this one uneven part by my nose that I can't stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try and make this spot on your face look better or to cope with the anxiety it causes you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like I mentioned, on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom to check the mirror. I just... I try to fix my hair so it falls around my face a certain way to cover it up. And I guess I also catch myself staring at the girls who come through my line, just comparing my skin to theirs. I get so caught up doing all that in the bathroom that I end up being late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about this spot on your face are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, like I was saying before about work, it just adds a lot of stress to my day. Knowing my manager sees me coming back late and moving slower at the register... it just makes me feel really anxious and down. I want that promotion so bad, and it really upsets me to think that worrying about this spot is making me look unreliable to them. It just kind of ruins my mood a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: Um, it really feels like I have to do it. Like I was saying before about going to the bathroom on my breaks... I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. But it's like I just can't stop myself. \n\nIf I try not to look, I just get too stressed out about how it looks. And it's the same with comparing myself to the girls in my line—I don't want to do it, but I just catch myself doing it anyway. So, yeah, it's not really something I can just turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you do to make sure you look okay, or is it more like you just can't help but do it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it's definitely more that I just can't help it. I mean, when I'm on my breaks, I always end up in the bathroom checking the mirror. I spend all that time trying to fix my hair so it hides the spot, and then when I'm back out there, I'm just constantly comparing myself to the girls coming through my line. I don't really want to be doing all that, especially since it makes me late getting back to the register, but I just... I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. Every break, I end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around the spot to hide it. And then I'm constantly comparing myself to the girls who come through my line. I don't want to be doing all that, especially because sometimes I take so long in the bathroom that I'm late getting back to the register, but the urge is just really strong.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I guess I've never really thought about it like that, because... well, I do worry about how it looks to people. If I somehow didn't care what others thought, maybe I wouldn't feel the need to check it so much? But it's hard to say, since the worry is always just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the spot on your face actually looks uneven to others, or is it just that you feel like it does?\n\nPatient: I mean, I know other people say they don't see it when I point it out. But to me, it's just so obvious. When I look at photos or when I'm under those bright store lights, it's all I can see. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? So yeah, I guess my anxiety would definitely go down if it was just fixed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if the spot were surgically corrected and you knew it looked even to others?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact it was fixed and everyone thought it looked even... I guess I wouldn't need to check it. But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much? But like I said, it's just such a habit now. I might just do it without even thinking about it, even if no one was around. I'm honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were convinced that the spot was completely even and normal-looking?\n\nPatient: I mean, if I actually believed it was completely normal... I guess I wouldn't need to look at it. But it's kind of hard to even imagine feeling that way right now. So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your need to check your face in the mirror is more about making sure you look okay, or is it something you feel compelled to do regardless of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. I'll spend time trying to fix my hair around it to hide the spot, or I'll just sit there comparing myself to the girls who come through my line at the register. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were under the impression that the spot had been completely removed?\n\nPatient: I think... if I really believed it was totally gone, I wouldn't need to check. But it's hard to say for sure, because I'm just so used to doing it at this point.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you check your face in the mirror because of how you feel about the spot, or because you feel an internal pressure to keep checking?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's really just because of how I feel about the spot. Like, I don't feel some random pressure to just stare into a mirror for no reason. It's always about that specific area and wanting to see how bad it looks, or if I can hide it better before I go back out to the register. So, yeah, it's definitely about the spot itself, not just an urge to check just to check.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So... yeah, I don't really know for sure what I'd do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the urge to check your face in the mirror if you were completely convinced that there was nothing wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's kind of the same thing... if I actually believed there was nothing wrong, I probably wouldn't feel the urge to check. But since I'm always at the register seeing people, or just catching my reflection in the store windows, it's hard to picture just turning off that urge completely. I'd like to think I wouldn't need to, but I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your need to check your face in the mirror is more about making sure you look okay, or is it something you feel compelled to do regardless of how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it's definitely about making sure I look okay, or trying to fix it so I look okay. Like, on my breaks at work, I always end up in the bathroom checking my face in the mirror. I'll spend time trying to fix my hair around it to hide the spot, or I'll just sit there comparing myself to the girls who come through my line at the register. Sometimes I get so caught up in checking and trying to fix it that I actually end up being late getting back to work. So yeah, it's really about trying to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror if you were under the impression that the spot had been completely removed?\n\nPatient: I think... if I really believed it was totally gone, I wouldn't need to check. But it's hard to say for sure, because I'm just so used to doing it at this point.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you check your face in the mirror because of how you feel about the spot, or because you feel an internal pressure to keep checking?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's really just because of how I feel about the spot. Like, I don't feel some random pressure to just stare into a mirror for no reason. It's always about that specific area and wanting to see how bad it looks, or if I can hide it better before I go back out to the register. So, yeah, it's definitely about the spot itself, not just an urge to check just to check.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't actually want to spend all that time checking the mirror and messing with my hair, especially because I know it makes me late getting back to the register. I can't seem to stop myself from doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you're worried about how you look, or because you feel a strong urge to do it even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I think it's kind of both, but like I was just saying, it's mostly that strong urge to do it even when I don't want to. I am worried about how it looks, but the checking is just something I can't seem to stop. So even if they say it's fine, I still feel like it's the very first thing customers and my managers notice when they look at me. That's actually why I came in... I just keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious or stressed if you couldn't check your face in the mirror, even if you didn't worry about how the spot looks to others?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, I've never really been in a situation where I wasn't worried about how it looks to other people. It's hard to imagine what I'd feel if that worry wasn't there. I guess maybe I wouldn't be as stressed if I couldn't check, but... yeah, I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease if the spot on your face were actually fixed or made even?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think so? If it was actually gone or completely even, I wouldn't have anything to worry about, right? But honestly, I've been doing it so much lately, like on my breaks at work, that it's kind of just a habit now. So I don't know if I'd just stop completely right away. I really couldn't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your face in the mirror even if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess if I was totally alone and knew nobody was coming over or anything... maybe I wouldn't need to check it as much?
